Lab: RF Management 
 
Implementation 
 1 
Objectives 
 
In the OmniVista Cirrus, the Radio Frequency settings management is done via “RF Profiles”. A RF Profile 
contains all the radio frequency settings assigned to the Stellar Access Points. Once created, it must be assigned 
to an AP or AP Group. 
 
1.1. 
POD Topology 
 
 
 
1.2. 
Access to OmniVista Cirrus  
Connect to the OmniVista Cirrus instance with the following URL: https://eu.manage.ovcirrus.com  
Login: pod##@ale-training.com , replace ## by your POD number [25-32] 
Password: Superuser01!

<<<PAGE 459>>>
4 
Lab: RF Management 
 
 2 
Creating a RF Profile 
2.1. 
General Settings 
A default RF profile exists and is assigned to the AP Group we have created earlier. 
 
- Under the category CONFIGURE, go to Wireless > Profile > RF Profiles. 
- Click on the button “+ Create RF Template”. 
- Enter the name “My_RF_Profile”. 
- Keep the country/Region “FR-France” (actual location of the Stellar AP).  
- Enter a description: “My custom RF profile”. 
- Click on Create. 
 
 
2.2. 
Smart Load Balance 
Smart Load Balance (SLB) is a feature that improves the user experience when accessing wireless 
connectivity by guiding a user's client device to connect to a free wireless channel or AP and denying 
access to APs with weak signal. 
2.2.1. 
Band Steering 
Band Steering controls the behavior of dual band clients and encourage them to use the 5 GHz band, 
which is generally less congested and provides higher speed. 
 
 
Warning > Why Band Steering is disabled by default? 
To function properly, band steering generally assumes that the coverage areas on both the 2.4 GHz bands and 5 
GHz bands are the same, or at least roughly equivalent. However, band steering will prove problematic if 
coverage on 5 GHz is significantly weaker and has coverage holes, as compared to coverage for 2.4 GHz. 
 
It can also cause problems. For example, a 5 GHz-capable device is automatically redirected to the 5 Ghz band 
by the band steering feature, even if the 5 GHz signal is low. 
 
Solution:  
- Design your networks for simultaneous 5 GHz and 2.4 GHz coverage.  
- For existing deployments where this may not be feasible, and your coverage is quite different on both bands, 
avoid using band steering or use the Exclude MAC OUI feature explained below.

<<<PAGE 460>>>
5 
Lab: RF Management 
 
 
2.2.2. 
Force 5G and 6G 
With force 5 or 6 GHz, a dual-band client device will only be allowed to connect to the network on the 5 
or 6 GHz band, and any requests to connect on the 2.4 GHz band will be ignored. This mode works quite 
well when the signal strength is good on the 5 or 6 GHz band but will prove problematic if there are weak 
coverage areas on 5 or 6 GHz because the network will not allow the client device to “fall back” to the 
2.4 GHz network. 
2.2.3. 
Exclude MAC OUI 
Excludes MAC OUI for band-steering (if Band Steering is enabled). The client will not utilize Band Steering 
and will be allowed to connect to the wireless band. This setting may be preferable for certain legacy and 
latency sensitive clients (e.g., scanners, MIPT Phones). 
2.2.4. 
Association RSSI Threshold 
This feature is used to set thresholds to optimize connectivity when associating with an AP by forbidding 
client access to networks with a weak wireless signal (RSSI, Received Signal Strength Indicator). Clients 
with an RSSI value lower that the Association RSSI Threshold will not be allowed to connect to the AP. 
 
 
- Find the RSSI value of your Wifi Client (we will consider in the lab that this RSSI 
value is too low to connect to the SSIDs created previously) 
- Modify the Association RSSI Threshold to make Wifi Client RSSI too low to connect 
the SSIDs created previously 
 
Find the Wifi Client signal strength Value. 
 
- Before doing this, be sure that the Raspberry Pi WifiClientX is connected to one of the SSIDs created in the 
previous labs! 
 
- Open a new tab, under the MONITOR category, select Network > Analytics > Clients  
-   Under the tab “Live Wireless Clients”, click on the Client MAC address in List. This will open a new 
tab in your web browser. 
-   Check (and note) the RSSI value (ex. -41 dBm/51dB).  
o 
Either at the top of the page :  
 
 
o 
Or from the RSSI graph: 
 
- Now, we are going to assume that the WifiClient signal strength (ex. -38 dBm) must be considered too 
weak to connect to the AP. To do so, we will set the Association RSSI Threshold to a value greater than

<<<PAGE 461>>>
6 
Lab: RF Management 
 
the client RSSI value. 
- Go back to Wireless > Profile > RF Profiles 
- Edit the profile My_RF_Profile 
- Scroll down to the Smart Load Balance section. 
- Modify the Association RSSI Threshold for all the bands to a value much higher than the Client value 
(ex. 90, which is higher than -41 dBm = 51) and click Save. 
 
 
 
 
Notes 
We will test this feature in the next section, as the RF Profile must be first applied to the desired AP or AP 
Group.  
 
2.2.5. 
Roaming RSSI Threshold 
This feature is used to set thresholds to optimize connectivity when roaming by forbidding client access to 
networks with a weak wireless signal (RSSI). Clients with an RSSI value lower than the Roaming RSSI 
Threshold value will be guided to roam to another AP with a better transmission signal. 
2.3. 
Per Band Info 
 
2.3.1. 
Default Setting 
Disable it to set custom bandwidth settings. Enable it to reset bandwidth settings to default values. 
2.3.2. 
Band 
Configures the working radio for the AP. 
2.3.3. 
Channel Setting 
Configures the working channel of the radio (auto = dynamically assigned via ACS, Auto Channel Selection) 
2.3.4. 
Client-aware 
When enabled, the Auto Channel Selection does not change channels for Stellar APs with connected client. 
When disabled, the Stellar AP may change to a more optimal channel but may disrupt connected clients. 
2.3.5. 
Channel DRM 
Enables/Disables the channel scope specification definition that will be applicable for Auto-Channel 
Selection. 
 
2.3.6. 
Channel List 
Specifies the channel list that will be applicable for Auto-Channel Selection. 
2.3.7. 
Channel Width 
Configures the channel width for 2.5 and 5G radio. Channel width is used to control how broad the signal 
is for transferring data. By increasing the channel width, you can increase the speed and throughput of a 
wireless broadcast. However, larger channel width brings more unstable transmission in crowded areas 
with a lot of frequency noise and interference. 
2.3.8. 
Power Setting 
Configures the transmit power of the wireless radio.

<<<PAGE 462>>>
7 
Lab: RF Management 
 
2.3.9. 
Minimum and Maximum TX Power 
Specify the minimum and maximum transmit power for auto power setting. 
2.3.10. External Antenna Gain 
Specify the gain value of the external AP antenna. Only the Stellar APs with external antennas (AP1222, 
AP1332,…) will be configured with this attribute. 
2.3.11. Beacon interval 
Beacon period for the AP. Indicates how often the 802.11 beacon management frames are transmitted by 
the AP. 
2.3.12. Short Guard Interval 
Guard Interval is used to ensure that distinct transmissions occur between the successive data symbols 
transmitted by a device. This would provide approximately an 11% increase in data rates. However, using 
the Short Guard Interval will result in higher packet error rates when the delay spread of the RF channel 
exceeds the Short Guard Interval, or if timing synchronization between the transmitter and receiver is not 
precise. 
2.3.13. MU-MIMO 
Enables/Disables Multi-User, Multiple-Input, Multiple-Output feature. If enabled, the AP can communicate 
with multiple users simultaneously. It decreases the time each device has to wait for a signal and speeds 
up the network. 
2.3.14. High Efficiency 
Enables/Disables 802.11ax high efficiency wireless feature. If disabled, a High Efficiency mode capable AP 
will downgrade to VHT (Very High Throughput) mode.

 3 
Assigning the RF Profile to an AP/AP Group  
3.1. 
Assigning the RF Profile to the AP Group 
 
- Under the category Configure, go to Wireless > Profile > Access Point Groups 
- Note the name of the “Provisioning Config” assigned to the AP Group My-AP-Group 
o 
It is “My-Provisioning-Config” 
The RF profile is contained in the Provisioning configuration of your AP Group. This is where we are changing 
it. 
- Go to Wireless > Profile > Provisioning Configuration 
- Select “MyProvisioning-Config” and click on the Edit button. 
- In the Basic Information section: 
o 
Set RF Profile to My_RF_Profile  
o 
Click on Save 
 
 
 
 
Notes 
Note that it is also possible to assign an RF Profile to a specific AP (instead of an AP Group). To do so, go to the 
Inventory > Device Catalog and select one of your Access Point. 
Select the Action Edit Device > Group / RF Profile. And change the parameter “RF Profile” with the new one 
created. 
 
 
Now that the RF Profile My_RF_Profile is applied to My-AP-Group, try to connect to an 
SSID from the Wifi Client.  
 
 
3.2. 
Connect to an SSID 
As the Wifi Client RSSI = 51dB is less than the Association RSSI Threshold = 90, then it is not possible for the 
wifi Client (and other devices with a RSSI less than 90) to connect to any SSID broadcasted by Access Points 
assigned to My-AP-Group.  
 
- Disconnect and reconnect you Wi-Fi client to one of your SSID (Ex: EmployeesX). 
The client tries to associate to the SSID but is not able to. The Stellar AP will ignore all association requests 
from the Wi-Fi client as the power of its signal is lower than the threshold.

<<<PAGE 464>>>
9 
Lab: RF Management 
 
3.3. 
Check the error in OmniVista Cirrus 
The error we have generated with the new RSSI threshold can be seen in the QoE analytics, error screens. 
- Go to Network > Analytics > QoE. 
- The first line of QoE metrics is the “Successful Connects”. Click on More details to analyze the association 
failure we encountered. 
 
- The “association” failures are selected by default. 
- At the bottom of the page, you can find the list of all the recent association failure: 
 
- Note the reason of the failure with the “Failure message” column. Or click on the “Additional 
information” button. 
 
3.4. 
Revert the RF Profile configuration 
In the current state, no Wi-Fi clients can connect to any of your SSID. 
Assign the default RF Profile back to the Provisioning Configuration associated to your AP Group: 
- Select Wireless > Profile > Provisioning Configuration 
- Select “My-Provisioning-Config” and click on the Edit button. 
- In the Basic Information section:  
o 
RF Profile: Default RF Profile  
o 
Click on Save 
During this lab, we have learned that the OmniVista Cirrus provides an easy way to manage the Stellar Access 
Points radio frequency settings.  
We have also learned that a lot of settings are available and can be enabled or disabled depending on the 
infrastructure deployed.

<<<PAGE 469>>>
OVERVIEW

<<<PAGE 470>>>
OVERVIEW
• Roaming relies on client context sharing between over the air adjacent APs
• L2 or L3 Roaming selection based on the client VLAN between "home" and "foreign" AP
• L3 Roaming based on L2 GRE tunnel between "home" and "foreign" AP
Fast Roaming
L2 Roaming
L3 Roaming
OmniVista Cirrus / Terra

<<<PAGE 471>>>
CONFIGURATION
• L2 Roaming always enabled
• L3 Roaming disabled by default
• L3 Roaming configured in the SSID Configuration 
• Fast Roaming disabled by default
• Fast Roaming feature configured per SSID
•
OKC can be enabled with WPA2/WPA3 Enterprise 
only
•
802.11r (Fast Roaming) can be enabled with
WPA2/WPA3 encryption only (Personal or Enterprise)

<<<PAGE 472>>>
CLIENT CONTEXT SHARING

<<<PAGE 473>>>
CLIENT CONTEXT SHARING
• AP Discovery Protocol
• Each AP learns about its “over-the-air” adjacent APs and their IP addresses
• No dependency on AP Groups and Management VLAN
• Limited to AP managed by the same OmniVista
• Client context shared with adjacent APs
• Over the LAN infrastructure
• IP based protocol 
• Add/Del Message
• On Client Association, AP sends a Add message to all adjacent APs
• On Client Dis-association, AP sends a Del message to all adjacent APs
• Upon Roaming, client context removal mechanism
• Del Message triggered on the “old” AP upon Add Message from the “new” AP

<<<PAGE 477>>>
FAST ROAMING

<<<PAGE 478>>>
FAST ROAMING
• Improves handoff times during roaming
• Removes RADIUS authentication
• Optimizes authentication handshake
• Requires key caching
• Support OKC (802.11k) and 802.11r 
• Configurable on the SSID Service
• OKC can be enabled with WPA2/WPA3 Enterprise only.
• It helps devices search for nearby APs that are available as roaming targets by creating an optimized list of 
channels
• 802.11r (Fast Roaming) can be enabled with WPA2/WPA3 encryption only (Personal or Enterprise)
• It uses a feature called Fast Basic Service Set Transition (FT) to authenticate more quickly in the network.
• If Fast Roaming is not enabled, then standard Roaming is used

<<<PAGE 479>>>
STICKY CLIENT AVOIDANCE

<<<PAGE 480>>>
STICKY CLIENT AVOIDANCE
• Goal: Optimize client distribution among APs
• In case of user roaming, suggest to the client to best new Access Point, based on availability and 
RSSI.
◼802.11v (BSS Transition Management): 
Obtain Roaming target APs
◼802.11k: Guide client to roam to best 
connection AP
◼Roaming RSSI: Guiding to roam threshold
⚫Located in the RF Profile

IDENTIFY THE ROAMING MODE
• Check the roaming conditions 
• Based on the VLAN ID between the "home" and 
"foreign" AP, select either:
• Layer 2 Roaming (default)
• Layer 3 Roaming
• Check the security level of the SSID 
(WPA/WPA2/WPA3, Enterprise/Personal)
• OKC can be enabled with WPA2/WPA3 Enterprise 
only
• 802.11r (Fast Roaming) can be enabled with
WPA2/WPA3 encryption only (Personal or 
Enterprise)

<<<PAGE 490>>>
CHECK THE RADIO COVERAGE
• Use the Heat Map application to check the radio coverage
• Select the 2.4GHz, 5GHz and 6GHz in the filters as they don't have exactly the same radio coverage
OK
Radio overlap, Roaming available
KO
No Radio overlap, no Roaming
No overlap
Overlap

<<<PAGE 491>>>
NEIGHBOR AP
• In some cases, Stellar APs are geographical 
neighbors but can’t see each other (i.e: radio waves 
blocked by corridor with right angles,…).
• Client context can't be shared. No roaming.
• Solution:
• On both AP, add statically the neighbor Stellar AP from 
the list of known AP.
• The client context can be shared through the LAN and 
the client can roam.
• Select the AP in the AP Registration > Access Point 
view and click on the hyperlink "Neighbor AP"
• Click on the Edit button and select the neighbor AP from 
the list
• Repeat the process for the second AP
No client 
context
sharing

<<<PAGE 492>>>
STICKY CLIENT AVOIDANCE
• The roaming decision is made by the client device. 
• But some devices will stick to the AP they were previously associated to.
• Use the Roaming RSSI Threshold in the RF profile.
• Use in conjuction with 802.11k and 802.11v
• Value range is 0-100
• Recommended value for 2.4GHz : RSSI = 10
• Recommended value for 5GHz : RSSI = 15
• The Roaming RSSI Threshold controls the signal strength a client needs to see before
searching for another site.
• If the RSSI threshold is too low, the client remains on a low signal strength site, even with a 
stronger site nearby.
• If the RSSI threshold is too high, the client roams too much that could result to packet loss.

<<<PAGE 513>>>
WIPS OVERVIEW
• Stellar APs monitors the radio 
spectrum for the presence of 
unauthorized
• AP
• Users 
• Automatically take countermeasures
• Global configuration applied to all APs 
managed by OV
• Require AP with scanning activated

<<<PAGE 514>>>
WIPS – INTERFERING / ROGUE / FRIENDLY AP
• Interfering AP
• Any other APs discovered over the air
• These APs are marked as Interfering
• APs managed by the same OVC 10 are excluded
• Rogue AP 
• Based on the Rogue AP Policy
• APs managed by the same OVC 10 are excluded
• Rogue AP Containment – enabled by default
• The scanning Stellar AP sends de-auth request to all 
clients associated to the rogue AP
• Friendly AP 
• Friendly AP is not reported as Interfering or 
Rogue 
• An Interfering or Rogue AP can be set as 
Friendly AP manually
• Friendly AP OUI can be set – ALE OUI set by 
default
• Friendly AP OUIs can be added

<<<PAGE 515>>>
WIPS – ROGUE AP POLICY
• If an interfering AP matches one of these Policies, it is classified as Rogue.
Policy
Description
Signal Strength Threshold
The detected AP signal in dbm is too strong and above the threshold
Default: – 70 dbm ; Range -50  to -90 dbm
Detect Valid SSID
The detected foreign AP is broadcasting a SSID that is configured in OmniVista Cirrus and set in your WLAN network.
The foreign AP is classified as Rogue
Detect Rogue SSID Keyword
The detected foreign AP is advertising a SSID name that matches a string set in this policy 
(SSID blocklist)
Rogue OUI
The interfering AP matching the configured MAC OUI is classified as Rogue

<<<PAGE 516>>>
WIPS – WIRELESS ATTACK DETECTION
• Enabled by default
• AP attack Detection Policy
• The scanning Stellar AP is detecting a wireless 
attack that seems to be originated from an AP
• Client Attack Detection Policy
• The scanning Stellar AP is detecting a wireless 
attack that seems to be originated from a client
• Set the detection level to:
• Custom
• High
• Medium
• Low

<<<PAGE 517>>>
WIPS – WIRELESS ATTACK CONTAINMENT
• Client Blocklist Policy
• Disabled by default 
• Puts the attacker source MAC in the client blocklist
• This MAC is not allowed to associate anymore on any of the Stellar AP managed by OmniVista Cirrus 
• A blocklist time duration is also configurable
• Limitations
• The attacker source MAC can be anything (an AP mac, a BSSID mac, a wireless NIC card mac..)
• Blocklisting the attacker source MAC is only relevant when the source MAC is an actual wireless client

<<<PAGE 518>>>
WIPS SUMMARY
• Rogue APs and Blocklist Clients
• Rogue Client Association provides an overview of detected Rogue APs and Clients. 
• Blocklist Clients provides an overview of clients that have been automatically and manually added 
to the Blocklist.

<<<PAGE 519>>>
WIPS SUMMARY
• Top N Attacks
• Displays a list of attacks from foreign APs and Clients based on the detection policy configured on 
OV Cirrus 10 / Terra

Lab: Wi-Fi Intrusion & Prevention System (WIPS) 
 
Implementation 
 1 
Objectives 
We are going to configure the WIPS and classify access points not managed by our OmniVista Cirrus. 
1.1. 
POD Topology 
 
1.2. 
Access to OmniVista Cirrus  
Connect to the OmniVista Cirrus instance with the following URL: https://eu.manage.ovcirrus.com  
Login: pod##@ale-training.com , replace ## by your POD number [25-32] 
Password: Superuser01!

<<<PAGE 523>>>
3 
Lab: Wi-Fi Intrusion & Prevention System (WIPS) 
 
 2 
WIPS Policy 
Access Points that can be seen by your Stellar Access Points managed in OmniVista Cirrus are either classified 
as Interfering, Rogue or Friendly. 
The users traffic going through the rogue access points can be the most impacted. 
We will see here how to detect a Rogue access point. 
2.1. 
Rogue AP Policy 
- Under the CONFIGURE section, go to Wireless > WIPS > Policy 
An Access Point that can be seen by the Stellar access points we manage, can be classified as a Rogue AP, 
thanks to the following conditions: 
 
 
Notes 
Do not modify the parameters, unless instructed to. 
Actions applied to Rogue AP can have big consequences to other wireless networks. 
 
- Signal Strength Threshold – Default: Disabled 
When the signal strength of a neighboring (or interfering) Access Point is greater than the threshold 
defined, the Access Point is considered too close to your own network and classified as Rogue AP.  
- Detect Valid SSID – Default: Enabled 
An interfering AP broadcasting the same SSID as yours in your Stellar network is classified as a Rogue AP.  
- Detect Rogue SSID Keyword – Default: Disabled 
An interfering AP broadcasting an SSID that contains the keyword entered here is classified as a Rogue AP.  
- Rogue OUI – Default: Disabled 
Interfering AP matching this MAC OUI are classified as Rogue AP.  
2.2. 
Friendly AP 
Any Access Point that is manually categorized as Friendly will never be classified as rogue. 
You can fill here a list of Stellar MAC OUI, that can be later classified manually as friendly AP. 
2.3. 
Containment Policy 
When Rogue AP containment is enabled, the rogue AP containment function reduces the impact of the rogue 
AP on valid clients. 
Technically, your Stellar Access Point sends a de-authentication packet to the Wi-Fi clients associated to 
rogue Access Points. This is why refining the classifications parameters of a Rogue AP is important. 
2.4. 
Wireless Attack Detection Policy 
A rogue AP is not the only threat to the wireless network. 
Other wireless attacks -originating from other Access Points and clients - can be detected and mitigated. 
2.4.1. 
AP Attack Detection Policy 
Select a level of detection or customize the list of policies used to detect attacks originating from Access 
Points. 
2.4.2. 
Client Attack detection Policy 
Select a level of detection or customize the list of policies used to detect attacks originating from wireless 
clients.

<<<PAGE 524>>>
4 
Lab: Wi-Fi Intrusion & Prevention System (WIPS) 
 
2.5. 
Client Blocklist policy 
When enabled, a wireless client can be placed in a blocklist if it matches the policies configured here: 
- The maximum authentication failure times. By default, if a client fails to authenticate 10 times in 60 
seconds, it will be placed in the blocklist. 
- And a client placed in a blocklist is removed from it after one day (Aging Time = 1 day by default). 
 3 
Intrusive Access Points 
In this page, all the neighboring Access Points, seen by your own Stellar Access Points, are listed in one of the 
three tabs: Interfering AP, Rogue AP and Friendly AP. 
- Rogue AP: Only the Access Points that have been matched by the rules defined in WIPS Policy are listed 
here. 
- Interfering AP: All the other neighboring Access Points are listed here. 
- Friendly AP: This list is empty by default, but can be filled manually by selecting an interfering AP and 
adding it to the Friendly AP. A Friendly AP will then never be categorized in the Rogue AP list. 
In addition, you can find the list of clients that are associated to the access points classified as Interfering 
and Rogue. 
When a client is selected, the only available action is to place this client in a blocklist. It means that you do 
not trust this client, and the next time it will try to associate to one of your SSID, the association request will 
be immediately rejected.  
3.1. 
Moving Stellar Access Points to the Friendly AP list 
- In the tab Interfering AP, enter the value “EmployeesX” in the research field (replace X by the POD 
number of another student) and press Enter. 
 
Notes 
We want here to add the two access points used by another student as Friendly AP.  
Make sure to use the correct filter -> SSID name from another POD, and not your own SSID name.  
- Select all the entries from the table that start with the Stellar MAC OUI “dc:08:56”.  
 
Notes 
Why multiple entries and not just 2? One line per Access Point.  
➔ 
The EmployeeX SSID is broadcasted on the 2.4G and 5G frequencies. Meaning that 2 BSSID are used 
per Access Point. So, 4 different BSSID in total, for the 2 Stellar APs broadcasting the SSID. And your 
two access points are scanning the air, this is why we have multiple similar entries. 
- Click on the button Action > Add to Friendly. 
 
- Confirm the action.    
- Go back to WIPS > Policy and note that the BSSIDs of your Stellar Access Points are in the Friendly MAC 
section.

<<<PAGE 525>>>
5 
Lab: Wi-Fi Intrusion & Prevention System (WIPS) 
 
3.2. 
Rogue policy impact on Friendly AP 
We have classified the Stellar Access Points from another POD as Friendly AP. 
It means that no Policy can now classify them as Rogue AP.  
This is what we will test here with a Policy based on a SSID Keyword. 
- In WIPS > Policy, check the box “Detect Rogue SSID Keyword”. 
- Enter the value ”EmployeesX”, which is exactly the same value as the one used earlier in part 3.1 
- Click on Save. 
 
 
Both Stellar AP broadcasting this SSID should be placed in the Rogue AP category. But as we have added these 
Access Points as Friendly, they will never be moved into Rogue AP. 
- Go to WIPS > Intrusive Access Point, ang go to the Rogue AP tab. 
You will notice that none of these Access Points are listed here. 
- Go back to your Wifi Client and connect to the SSID “EmployeesX” (another student SSID). 
You are still able to connect to the SSID.  
 
 
Notes 
The other student, managing this SSID, can check your client status, under Network > Analytics > Clients. 
 
- On your OmniVista Cirrus instance, Go to Network > Analytics > WIPS Analytics. 
- Under the tab Access Points, note that the Access Point marked as Friendly just earlier is not present 
in the Rogue AP Detected list.  
- And under the Clients tab, you shall not see the MAC address of your Wi-Fi client in the DeAuth Client 
Details at the bottom of the page. 
You have learned about the Access Point classification as Interfering, Friendly, Rogue AP and WIPS Policy. 
Now you have completed this lab.

• Identify Access Point model : same as original design?
• Identify RF overlap between Access Points : Co/Adjacent channel interference?
• Identify areas with no radio coverage : Access Point down? No Access Point placed?
• Access Point transmission power: Default or customized value?
• Access Point location: Troublesome placement?

Lab: Organization clean up 
 
Implementation 
 1 
Objectives 
 
We are going to clean the different parameters, profiles and accounts created during this training. 
As OmniVista Cirrus is cloud-based, it is not possible to revert the configuration back to the default parameters 
with one click. 
 
You can use this lab as a guideline if you need to replace your network devices, move to a new office or 
building, or reconfigure your network. 
In this lab, we will undo the configuration performed during this training. 
Then, for your use case, you can reconfigure your network, starting with the first labs.   
1.1. 
POD Topology 
 
 
 
1.2. 
Access to OmniVista Cirrus  
Connect to the OmniVista Cirrus instance with the following URL: https://eu.manage.ovcirrus.com  
Login: pod##@ale-training.com , replace ## by your POD number [25-32] 
Password: Superuser01!

<<<PAGE 545>>>
4 
Lab: Organization clean up 
 
 2 
Organization Clean up 
 
You can follow the next points as a how to clean your organization: 
2.1. 
Remove Upgrade 
- Go to: Inventory > Backup /Upgrade 
- In the Scheduled Upgrades tab, select and Action > Delete any upgrade planned. 
2.2. 
Remove Backups 
- Go to: Inventory > Backup /Upgrade 
- In the Configuration Backups tab, select and Action Delete all the Devices from the list. 
2.3. 
Remove Device Troubleshooting 
- Go to: Inventory > Device Troubleshooting 
- Select and Action Delete all the assigned commands from the list. 
2.4. 
Reset WIPS policy 
- Go to: Wireless > WIPS > Policy 
- Click on the button “Apply to default”, and then, confirm with the “reset” button. 
2.5. 
Un-assign custom AP Group 
- Go to: Inventory > Device Catalog. 
- Select both Access Points and click on Action > Edit Devices > Group/RF Profile. In the new window, select 
the AP Group “default device group” and click Confirm. 
2.6. 
Un-assign provisioning configuration 
- Go to: Wireless > Profile > Access Point Groups 
- Select and Edit the group My-AP-Group. 
- In the new window, select “Default Provisioning Config” in the Provisioning Configuration field and click 
on Save. 
2.7. 
Delete your custom AP Group 
- In the same page, select and delete My-AP-Group. 
- When you are asked to select a new AP Group for your Access Points, select “default device group” and 
click on Delete”. 
 
 
Notes 
The AP Group can only be deleted if no custom provisioning configuration is assigned. 
2.8. 
Delete your custom provisioning configuration 
- Go to: Wireless > Profile > provisioning configuration 
- Select and Delete “My-Provisioning-Configuration”. 
- If you get an error while trying to delete it, Edit this profile and set the RF profile parameter with 
“Default RF Profile”.

<<<PAGE 546>>>
5 
Lab: Organization clean up 
 
2.9. 
Delete your custom RF Profile 
- Go to: Wireless > Profile > RF Profile 
- Select and Delete “My_RF_Profile”. 
2.10. Delete the SSIDs 
- Go to: Wireless > SSIDs 
- Select all SSIDs and delete them. 
2.11. Delete your custom Unified Policy 
- Go to: Network Access > Unified Access > Unified Policies 
- Select and Delete “Block_SSH”. 
2.12. Delete your custom Service Port “SSH” 
- Go to: Network Access > Groups > Service Ports 
- Select and Delete the service called “SSH (22)” (TCP port 22 in the filters). 
2.13. Delete your custom BYOD Access Strategy 
- Go to: Network Access > UPAM-NAC > BYOD Access Strategy 
- Select and Delete “My_BYOD_Strategy”. 
2.14. Delete your ARP “Employee_BYOD#” 
- Go to: Network Access > Unified Access > Access Role Profile 
- Select and Delete “Employee_BYOD#”. 
2.15. Delete your custom Guest Access Strategy 
- Go to: Network Access > UPAM-NAC > Guest Access Strategy 
- Select and Delete “Guest_OV#”. 
2.16. Delete your custom Captive Portal template 
- Go to: Network Access > UPAM-NAC > Captive Portal Templates 
- Select and delete the template called “MyCPTemplate#”. 
2.17. Delete your Employee account 
- Go to: Network Access > Accounts > Employee Accounts 
- Select and delete the account “Employee”. 
2.18. Delete your Guest account 
- Go to: Network Access > Accounts > Guest Accounts 
- Select and delete the account “Guest”. 
2.19. Delete your custom Report 
- Go to: Network > Reports 
- Select and delete the report “Report#”.

<<<PAGE 547>>>
6 
Lab: Organization clean up 
 
2.20. Delete your custom Support info collection 
- Go to: Diagnostic Tools > Collect Support Info 
- Select and delete all the support info files. 
2.21. Delete your custom Site 
- Go to: Sites Management > Sites 
- Delete the site “My Site”. 
 
 
Notes 
This action deletes the site, the building and floors attached, as well as all the network devices assigned to the 
site. The next step is a verification step. 
2.22. Confirm the removal of your network devices 
- Go to: Inventory > Device Catalog 
- Check that the page is empty. 
- If Network devices are still present, select them and delete them. 
2.23. Delete your custom CLI Templates 
- Go to: LAN > CLI Based Provisioning 
- Go to the tab: Templates 
- Select all the templates and delete them. 
2.24. Delete your custom Value Mappings 
- In the same page, go to the tab: Value Mappings 
- Check that the Value Mappings are already deleted with the previous action. 
- If not, select all the templates and delete them. 
2.25. Final status 
 
After the completion of this lab, you have now removed all your custom configuration and are now ready to 
add new devices, create new SSIDs, user accounts, … 
 
Here is what your Device Catalog looks like:

<<<PAGE 548>>>

Setting to complete this 
process. 
 
VPN Settings: Name  
User-configured name for the VPN configuration. 
Server's Public IP 
The VPN Server's Public IP address (configured when you installed the VPN VM). This is the IP 
address used by Remote APs to connect to the VPN Server. And this is the interface through 
which traffic originating from inside the Enterprise Network flows to the Remote site. 
Port 
The VPN Server Port. 
Server's VPN IP 
The VPN Server's Private IP address (configured when you installed the VPN VM). This is the 
interface through which traffic originating from the Remote AP flows to reach a destination 
inside the Enterprise Network. 
OmniVista Enterprise Server 
IP 
The IP address of the OmniVista 2500 NMS that will manage the devices. 
Client VPN IP Address Pool 
The range of addresses available to assign to Remote APs. You can select IP range and insert 
a range of IP addresses, or a shorthand mask.

Lab: Remote Access Point (RAP) Deployment – OVC 10 & OV2500 NMS 
 
Name  
User-configured name for the VPN configuration. 
Server's Public IP 
The VPN Server's Public IP address (configured when you installed the VPN VM). This is the IP 
address used by Remote APs to connect to the VPN Server. And this is the interface through 
which traffic originating from inside the Enterprise Network flows to the Remote site. 
Port 
The VPN Server Port. 
Server's VPN IP 
The VPN Server's Private IP address (configured when you installed the VPN VM). This is the 
interface through which traffic originating from the Remote AP flows to reach a destination 
inside the Enterprise Network. 
Client VPN IP Address Pool 
The range of addresses available to assign to Remote APs. You can select IP range and insert 
a range of IP addresses, or a shorthand mask.

Tips

7.1. 
Creating an Employee SSID

Notes

> Select WLAN > SSIDs > SSIDs

    > Click on Create & Customize 
 
    > Allowed Band: 2.4GHz and 5GHz 
    > Encryption Type: WPA3_AES  
 
Authentication Strategy 
> RADIUS Server: UPAMRadiusServer 
> Click on Manage Employee Accounts
